CHRISTINA WARREN: Okay. Well, that was a fantastic conversation with Mike Volpi. And as you were kind of, you know, discussing the – before your interview with him, his background, I mean, is so fascinating because as you mentioned, he had this experience at Cisco where he was for 14 years. And as you mentioned, you know, it was kind of the center of kind of, you know, the dot-com kind of boom and kind of the first real internet web boom, and continues to be very successful.

And what really struck me though was, you know, you have this person who’s obviously brilliant, goes to Stanford, studies mechanical engineering, and, you know, was working in engineering at Cisco, and then says, I want to learn about the business.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah.

CHRISTINA WARREN: And goes and gets an MBA.

I – I’ve met a lot of, you know, entrepreneurs, I’ve met a lot of technologists. I know a number of MBAs, I – it’s fairly uncommon. Has that been your experience? I don’t know a ton of people who have had previous, you know, interest in the business side, who are, you know, engineering majors, who’ve decided, no, I want to go get my MBA.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, like, there’s some. I mean like, quite famously my boss is one of them.

But, you know, it is an interesting thing, like, particularly at that point in time when you are sitting, you know, you’re sort of sitting in Silicon Valley at one of the boom moments. And, you know, maybe that’s even a weird thing to say because Silicon Valley has more or less been booming for, you know, five decades plus now.

But, you know, the ‘80s and ‘90s when he was like an undergrad and grad student were like an especially active time in Silicon Valley. And, you know, I know a lot of people who just don’t even want to get an undergraduate degree because they’re afraid that they’re going to miss out on something.

And so, you know, I think it’s one of the things that I really do respect about Mike is that his curiosity is broad versus narrow. And so, like, he’s just constantly trying to fill in the gaps in his experience so that he can give himself even more permission to be more curious. And I think that’s what going to B-school was was about.

CHRISTINA WARREN: Oh, totally.

KEVIN SCOTT: It’s him, you know, like being an engineer and realizing, okay, well, like, engineering without context is not super interesting. So, like, let me go figure out how to get context.

And then, you know, as we heard, you know, the more curious you are, like I think the more opportunities you create for yourself because, you know, him being at B-school is the thing that created the connection for him to go to Cisco.

CHRISTINA WARREN: Right. Right. No, which again, I mean, I think is so fascinating, right, that he had that curiosity and that kind of instinct to go to the next place, which has clearly set him up for all the stages in his career, both at Cisco doing the, you know, investment and biz dev stuff, and then as a CEO and now as an investor.

I really enjoyed the conversation that the two of you had about being too early versus too late because I think that that’s one of those conversations that people could have kind of endlessly for hours, right?

But I did kind of want to ask you; what was it about AI in this moment – let me be more specific – this moment in AI that we’re having, this moment, you know, with transformers and with generative AI and whatnot, that made you make the case to make some of the investments that Microsoft has made and to be one of the, you know, earlier, you know, people who’s kind of involved with that?

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, for me, it was that I saw in 2017 really, 2016, 2017, that these systems were really doing interesting things as they scaled up, and that they behaved more like a platform with greater scale.

And so, one of the things that I had been skeptical about with AI and, you know, and I have been episodically skeptical about AI for most of my career. So when I was an undergraduate and in grad school, we were in an AI winter. So like, we had in the, you know, early mid-’80s, like people got super enthusiastic about AI and, you know, like lots of energy and lots of people studying it and lots of companies forming. And we – like we had a really catastrophic bust.

And this has happened multiple times, like where you’ve had moments of enthusiasm and then, you know the air goes out of the bubble. It’s so frequent, in fact, you can go search for AI Winter and there’s a Wikipedia article explaining the bust part of the cycle.

And so, you know, the thing that I’d had skepticism about for a while is like, if the problem you’re trying to solve with AI is like the intellectual exercise of understanding in very precise terms the mechanism of intelligence, which is what a lot of AI had been about. It’s like, okay, like we’ve got this brain. It must be like this subtle symbol processing system. And, you know, just like, you know, Newton’s Laws of motion and Einstein’s theory of relativity, like, you know, there’s some, you know, mathematical beauty sitting, you know, in the neurons and axons in your brain. And, you know, like, if we just sort of think about it hard enough, like we self–reflect, like we’re going to find these pure, simple rules that explain how cognition works.

I was super skeptical that that was going to be a thing that goes fast. You know, because the nature of scientific breakthroughs like that is, you know, it’s just sort of slow. Like, you go slow and then you have a breakthrough and things, you know, change. But, you know, predicting progress is hard.

And what happened in 2017, like particularly with transformers, is like we had a mechanism whereby things could go fast. And like, you didn’t actually have to understand the fundamental nature of human cognition. You had this thing that, as you applied scale in data and compute, could emulate parts of human cognition, and the parts that it could emulate were useful enough where you could build a platform on top of it.

And so, like that’s, you know, was a pretty straightforward argument, like it just seemed blindingly obvious. And, you know, if you are a company like Microsoft that is a technology platform company, and like your job is to equip people to go solve problems with technology, like if this is going to become a platform, you must make sure that it is a great, excellent, you know, part of the overall platform that you’re building. Like, you can’t really have a technology platform, I think, in 2024 without having highly capable AI.

CHRISTINA WARREN: No definitely not. But certainly that wasn’t clear to many people in 2017, except for people like yourself and people like Mike, who were, you know, already investing in companies even before some of the seminal papers were being written.

So as always, like I’m appreciative of both of your insights because I think we’ve obviously needed this, especially as Mike was pointing out, this has become only more capital intensive. And, you know, kind of that early era is it’s kind of probably a generational thing where, you know, there are very few of, when I look back in history, like moments to kind of get in at that same stage, and now it’s just about identifying, you know, the next big waves probably associated with these things.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, and there’s still, I mean, the thing I will say to everyone who’s listening there, there’s still like an unspeakable amount of stuff to be done. It’s just, you know, like what folks ought to be thinking about right now is like, how do I go harness the power of this platform to go build amazing new things to solve the education problems and the healthcare problems and the just myriad business problems that you’re going to be able to solve now that you’ve got a new tool in your toolbox to go after them.

So like, that’s to me, you know, the fun stuff. Like, I always enjoy, like I derive a whole bunch of satisfaction in building products for other people building products and – but – but like, at the end of the day, like building tools is kind of useless unless people use the tools to do something that matters.

CHRISTINA WARREN: It’s so true. Like, yeah for platforms to work, people have to actually use them and want to build things off of them. And I think we’re lucky we’re in a space right now where people are definitely doing amazing things and are going to continue to do amazing things. And people like Mike are going to continue to invest in those things, and which is great.

KEVIN SCOTT: For sure.

CHRISTINA WARREN: All right, well, that is all the time that we have for today. Huge thanks to Mike Volpi for joining us.

If you have anything that you would like to share, you can email us at BehindtheTech@Microsoft.com. And you can follow Behind the Tech on your favorite podcast platform, or you can check out full video episodes on YouTube. Thanks for listening.

KEVIN SCOTT: See you next time.

END


# Ep-65_Behind_the_Tech_Kevin Scott_Podcast_with_Ethan_Mollick

Behind the Tech with Kevin Scott
Episode 65 – Ethan Mollick

ETHAN MOLLICK: Like, when I talk to companies, I always tell them two things. One thing is that something you used to do that was valuable is no longer valuable. And the sooner you realize that, the better off you are, right? And the second thing is, there’s something impossible you always wanted to do that you can do. What is the impossible thing?

KEVIN SCOTT: Hi, everyone. Welcome to Behind the Tech. I’m Kevin Scott, Chief Technology Officer and EVP of AI at Microsoft.
Today, tech is part of nearly every aspect of our lives. We’re in the early days of an AI revolution promising to transform our lived experiences as much as any technology ever has. On this podcast, we’ll talk with the folks behind that technology and explore the motivations, passion, and curiosity driving them to create the tech shaping our world. Let’s get started.

[MUSIC]

CHRISTINA WARREN: Hello and welcome to Behind the Tech. I’m co-host Christina Warren, senior developer advocate at GitHub.

KEVIN SCOTT: And I’m Kevin Scott.

CHRISTINA WARREN: And we have a fascinating interview today with Ethan Mollick, who is a professor at the Wharton School and who’s at the forefront of studying AI and especially its impact on work and education.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, I think Ethan is an incredibly energetic person, you know, just sort of thinking about how these new tools can be used to solve interesting problems. And you know, he has accumulated quite a following on social media, as well as the excellent work that he was doing in academic circles, just talking not just rigorously about how AI has all of these incredible potential benefits, but, you know, just really engaging on like, hey, like, I’m trying this, like, you know, you all should have a look and just giving people that provocation to be a little bit more ambitious with how they’re thinking about the tools. So like, he – he’s, you know, one of, in my opinions, the most interesting voices out there right now talking about AI.

CHRISTINA WARREN: No, I totally agree, and I’m really, really looking forward to hearing this conversation.

KEVIN SCOTT: Ethan Mollick is an associate professor at the Wharton School of the University of Pennsylvania. He teaches innovation and entrepreneurship, and his research examines AI’s impact on work and education, among other things. His papers have been published and cited in top journals, and his book on AI, Co-Intelligence, is a New York Times bestseller.

Ethan writes a newsletter on Substack called One Useful Thing, where he shares his understanding of the implications of AI for work, education and life, with almost 150,000 subscribers.

Ethan also leads Wharton’s Interactive, an effort to democratize education through using games, simulations and AI.

Prior to his time in academia, Ethan co-founded a startup company, and he advises numerous organizations.

Ethan received his PhD and MBA from MIT Sloan School of Management and his bachelor’s degree from Harvard University.

Ethan, we’re so happy to have you on the podcast today. Thanks for being here.

ETHAN MOLLICK: I’m thrilled to be here. Thanks for having me.

KEVIN SCOTT: So the way these conversations on my podcast always go is we sort of start at the very beginning. You know, how did you get interested in technology in the first place? And like, what was your childhood like, presuming that, you know, the interest in tech started that early?

ETHAN MOLLICK: I know this is going to turn out to be a complete shock to you, but I have been a long time nerd of the old school category, right? So I had my MS-DOS machines, and I had, you know, we were, you know, trading games among ourselves. I ran a bulletin board for a little while back in the 80s, and then have been sort of tech adjacent ever since. But, you know, video games and in the form they existed in the ‘80s, programing stuff. I’m not actually a coder, but I’ve been coder adjacent my entire life, so.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah.

ETHAN MOLLICK: That – that’s – so definitely the background.

KEVIN SCOTT: And so it is an interesting thing. So you know, you and I are probably, like I presume I’m older than you, but, you know, we were sort of coming up during that ascent of personal computing into basically just ubiquity in the public. So we basically went from, you know, computers were things that sat in rooms, were horrendously expensive, you know, capital intensive devices and only, you know, like very strange people with a very high degree of focus used them, to like, everyone had one, and like, the transition happened really quickly.

So, you know, and I want to talk about the parallels from then and now a little bit later. But what – what was it, you know, in your family that even gave you the exposure? Because, you know, running BBSs and doing all of this stuff was still, you know, back in the ‘80s, that – that was a pretty advanced technical set of things to be doing.

ETHAN MOLLICK: So, I mean, part of this was I still remember when my we had a neighborhood computer club. So everyone got together and bought Apple IIEs, and then we shared software because it was expensive. So you’d get a disk and you could borrow Print Shop or whatever.

So there was – it kind of gave me a built-in community, right? So this is before the days of the internet, but all the other kids in my neighborhood, where we were all trading the same disks back and forth and doing that.

So there was this kind of like, built-in community piece among nerdy kids, right, which is suddenly all the weird nerd hobbies you have, like unlock your ability to play video games and do all this kind of other fun stuff.

So it was – I grew up in Milwaukee, Wisconsin, like not a coastal person at all, even though I sound like a New Yorker. But you know, it was interesting because it was just something that was local. And you’d buy your – you know, your computer magazines, and it felt like there was a community among fellow high school and, you know, middle school students that kind of drove it.

KEVIN SCOTT: And – so you had all of these nerd interests, and what did you choose to do? Like what was sort of, you know, your path, like what you were super interested in, in high school? Like, you know, you went to college. Like, what did you choose to major in and why?

ETHAN MOLLICK: So I made up my own majors in undergrad in science technology policy. So the thing about – the horrible secret about me is people think I’m really, really good at sort of the math and science. I got a PhD from MIT. I am actually – you know, but I’m not actually a natural math person, right? What I am pretty good at is understanding systems and how they relate together. And I’m very, you know, understand science deeply, but I’m not the kind of cutting-edge math person out there, right?

So I’ve always been very interested in what does science mean and how to use it and understanding deeply how it works. And so, I made up a major in science and technology policy, history of science. I wrote – my original paper I ever wrote was – my undergraduate thesis was on Moore’s Law and interviewed Gordon Moore to try and figure out –

KEVIN SCOTT: That’s awesome.

ETHAN MOLLICK: – actually my first academic paper that was ever published was sort of why this happened the way it was, and that’s sort of – and a lot of – I did work with the Media Lab at MIT at the same time. So very interesting kind of what it all means, rather than trying to think about coding myself.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, well, I mean, maybe we can just sort of jump right into what it all means because some of the things, you know, that we’ve already talked about, like this, the PC revolution, like this point in time where a brand new technology went from inaccessible and unusable to like, highly usable and ubiquitous seems like it’s playing out again right now.

And like, one of the things that drove that earlier round of innovation is Moore’s Law, the – the fact that the compute got cheap so quickly that, you know, you could do increasingly interesting things with computers and, you know, even, you know, go through the process of making them more useful, like you could burn part of the CPUs resources on things like user interface.

And it’s sort of the same thing right now. Like, we’ve got AI. Like, it’s very – you know, it’s made the phase shift from, you know, really challenging technology to go leverage to very easy technology to leverage, and it’s being largely driven by compute. So, you know, like I love your take on what the parallels are.

ETHAN MOLLICK: There’s a second dimension besides compute. I mean, compute is the underlying driver of almost everything, right? Like, you look behind – you know, you have the Scooby-Doo mask of almost anything, and it’s Moore’s Law behind it, right, which is like, that’s the driving force of the last century. And so, you know – or three-quarters of a century at this point. And, you know, always an interesting topic about how that kind of existed.

But the interesting thing that also drives this is the kind of unexpected fact that LLMs especially turn out to be a very human technology. It turns out by training them on human stuff, they just work in a very human way.

And that means that people who can’t code, like, we’re back to that kind of interesting world where you don’t have to know magical coding secrets. If you’re a good manager of people, you’re probably going to be good with LLMs.

So there’s kind of this leapfrog effect also, which is most of the technologies start with, like how do you hack the command line, and then it takes a long time to get to the point where it’s like, okay, there’s a GUI and you don’t even have to know what a file system is anymore.

That’s not happening with LLMs, right? The interesting skip is the most useful way to do this, you know, there’s some early evidence that coders are actually the worst people to use AI because it doesn’t do loops. It doesn’t do any of the things you would expect it to do in code.

AI, you know, your computer system shouldn’t be probabilistic. It shouldn’t argue with you and try and diagnose your psychological issues. AI does those things when you interact with it.

So I think part of what makes the skip interesting is not just its accessibility, but the fact that it’s a kind of a new modality by accident of working with machines.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, it’s sort of interesting that you point that out. It’s a thing I’ve been saying for a handful of years now. We – for the first two centuries, really of programing, basically the only way that you could get a computing device to do your bidding was either become an expert programmer, which was this very complicated task of, you know, learning the intricacies of the machine and then learning all of the ways in which you can map human understanding of problems onto things that the machine can actually go execute. Or you have to rely on a programmer to have anticipated your needs and built a program that you can then go run on one of these computing devices. And like, that’s sort of the way it was since, you know, Ada Lovelace wrote the first program.

And like, that has very dramatically changed in the past handful of years. You can now get machines to do very, very complicated things for you where you are not constrained by having to be a very skilled programmer, which is not to say skilled programing is like a thing that’s going away. Like, maybe we need even more of them than we ever did, but it like really opens the aperture up on who gets to decide what computing devices go do.

ETHAN MOLLICK: And I think that – and by the way, I think that one of the most interesting things is actually the role Microsoft is playing with Copilot and its wide availability around the world.

You know, when I talk to organizations, I’m like, listen, you know, Goldman Sachs or Coca-Cola or whatever, I’m like, you know, you’re used to having the most advanced technology, and you need a whole bunch of sort of techno priests to manage it. And, you know, you hire my advanced PhDs from Wharton to run it, and you have the consultants who could afford to build the backend systems.

But the AI that you’re using internally is almost certainly worse than what every kid in Mozambique has access to for free, thanks to Microsoft’s Copilot and, you know, running GPT-4, like and that people have not absorbed that completely yet, right, like also that the technological frontier just caught up to everybody at the same time, and there isn’t really a huge advantage. Like companies are shipping models almost as fast as they can get them made out. They’re generally made pretty ubiquitously available or available, at relatively cheap costs most places around the world. And the implications of that are going to play out in really interesting ways that we haven’t anticipated yet.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah. And so, you know, maybe that’s a good segue to your book. So you’ve written this book, Co-Intelligence. What do you want people most to take away, you know, from reading this book and just in general from, you know, how they ought to be thinking about what’s going on right now?

ETHAN MOLLICK: So at least at the current stage, AI really works like a form of co-intelligence. Like, it is a booster to your activities. It is a threat to some parts of your job, but not the parts you want to do. And it is something that is usable right now.

And I think a lot of people and a lot of the books about AI have tended to focus on future, and especially sort of scary versus, you know, like, are we all saved or all doomed? And I think that that is an important conversation, but in some ways, the least interesting conversation to have about AI that’s already here.

And it’s fascinating because when you talk to people who are using it, they want to talk about how to use it. It feels like the 80s again, right? Like, people want to figure out what are the tips. They’re exchanging information. There’s excitement in the air among users.

And I think that I wanted to try and bring that conversation to people and give people ways of getting started. And also to realize, like, this is kind of a big deal, right? It’s a big deal in lots of ways that we would never have expected AI to be a big deal. And it’s a big deal right now. Like, it out-innovates most innovators. It out-writes most writers. Like, you know, elite consultants, it does a really good job. Like, this is weird stuff that is going to have weird effects. And it is accessible. And that’s part of why it’s gonna have such a weird effects.

KEVIN SCOTT: Well, I’d love to talk a little bit about benefits, and then we can sort of talk about, you know, maybe the weird downside effects in a minute. But, you know, I’ve been doing machine learning work for 20 years now, and yeah, and have been on the large language model train for, you know, the past, you know, five-plus years, you know, helping OpenAI build their big systems.

And like, there’s a handful of things that are, like, just super clear that I think are going to be massive beneficiaries of the technology. And like, they tend in my mind to be things where you have zero sum problems that exist, where the AI like comes in and transforms all of the problem or some important part of it into a non-zero-sum thing. And there are many, many examples in education and health care, I think.

But like, what are your like, interesting benefits right now, like the things that you’re super excited about and think people ought to be thinking more about?

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yeah, on the large scale social side, I think you’re completely right. I mean, the standard I’ve been applying, AI has many things it doesn’t do very well. And there’s things that people worry a lot about how well it does them. But the standard I tend to apply is BAH, Best Available Human. Is the AI better or worse than the best available human you have access to? And especially in areas like education where most people don’t have access to tutors, but that’s a magical tool, that’s transformative, right?

For areas like, you know, medical advice, like we’re getting good data out of both Microsoft and Google and Harvard Medical School and everyone else right now. They’re like, look, it’s – you know, I don’t want to replace your doctor, but you probably should be asking for a second opinion from AI and getting help. And your doctor probably should be doing that at least, right. It’s pretty good at legal stuff. Like, there’s a lot of things where access has been gated, where this opens the doors, for reasons good and bad. Education is clearly a giant win.

The other thing is like, look, when we survey people who use AI, we get the same results almost every time, which is they’re nervous because will this take my job, etcetera, but they’re actually really happy because the first things they give up are the worst things, right? AI maxes out right now at the 80th percentile of ability. A lot of things, it’s much less than that. And – but whatever you’re best at, you’re probably better than an AI. But there’s probably parts of your job that actually suck that you’re not very good at and that you don’t enjoy.

And so, the most liberating part of this is like, people are bored 25% of the time at work in most surveys. The idea that you can kind of give up the parts of your job that are holding you back, and especially as an entrepreneurship professor, the stuff you’re not very good at, the stuff that takes all your time and energy, even though it doesn’t get very far, like that’s fascinating.

I mean, I find it fascinating that the first use almost everyone tells me they put AI to, are actually the most intimate uses, like writing children’s stories for their kids, writing eulogies and wedding toasts, because these are things that cause people enormous amounts of stress, and most people aren’t that good at, right? And so, all they do is spend hours and hours stressed out about it. So to me, it’s this liberating aspect also on the personal level, as well as what it can do to society.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, I like that Best Available Human framework because I think it’s a good framing to help you think about how you could ambitiously use it yourself. So, like what is the thing that you want to do or that you feel constrained by, that you need some help with, and like can you get a human to help you, or can you not because, like, you can’t afford to pay them or because, like, they don’t live near you or they’re inaccessible?

Like my example of this is my 15-year-old daughter, who, you know, is a freshman and was taking a biochemistry class in high school this year. And she really, really wanted to read the research literature. And the research literature is not written for 15-year-old kids. It’s like full of jargon. It’s complicated. It presumes a bunch of prior knowledge that you don’t have when you’re 15 years old.

But conceptually, like, you know, if the research is good, there’s no reason that a 15-year-old can’t understand the kernel of what’s in a research paper.

And so, like, her idea is like, all right, I’m just going to feed this thing into ChatGPT and just ask ChatGPT to explain this thing to me, and just, you know, like, I’m going to pepper ChatGPT with questions. And like, she doesn’t have access to a PhD biochemist who can, like, personally tutor her, you know, through her million questions she’s got about these random papers.

But ChatGPT is like the free version. Like, she didn’t even ask me to do this, right? So it’s like she’s using the free tier of the product, and like, it’s almost like watching a kid develop a superpower. Like, I cannot believe what she understands.

ETHAN MOLLICK: I mean, it’s amazing, right? I mean, part of what – you know, I think it was interesting because we thought that, you know, going back to sort of the history of the thing, I think most of us who were nerds in the sort of ‘80s and ‘90s thought that once we had the universal network, that everyone had access to all information, everybody would be seeking out the information they want, and the world would become wise overnight, right? And it turned out to not quite work the way I think the teenagers of the ‘80s and ‘90 nerds thought it would.

And I think part of the issue is that we didn’t understand society and we didn’t understand that people have different styles and need different amounts of knowledge and need things to be applicable in their life and different levels of curiosity about different things.

And one of the most exciting things about AI is universal translator and not just translator of language, which it does pretty well, but translator of concepts, right? It’s like the weakest way to do that is explain it like I’m 10. The best way to do it is to tell the AI, hey, I am, you know, I’m a 15-year-old, and here’s what my background, and here’s what I’ve studied, and here’s my interest. Can you explain this paper to me in a way that I’d understand, and that it seems relevant to me, and you’ll get a good answer from that.

And to me, that is a superpower for all of us, right, because it turns out that just providing access to unfiltered knowledge is not the right way to go. We need help understanding and contextualizing, making it meaningful. And I think that’s incredibly powerful.

KEVIN SCOTT: So you teach entrepreneurship to very bright young people, and probably old people, too, right, like who I’m guessing you have all sorts of folks in your classes. But, you know, folks who have this curiosity about how the cycle of entrepreneurship works, presumably because they want to go be entrepreneurs or like, participate in the entrepreneurial process.

So one of the things that’s most exciting to me, when these big technological platform shifts happen is like the opportunities for entrepreneurship are pretty incredible. I don’t know what you are advising your students to do. Like, you know, how to – you know, like how are you helping them take advantage of the moment or advising them what to do?

ETHAN MOLLICK: There’s a lot to talk about there. So we’ll start in the most narrow front, right? Like, I’m a former entrepreneur myself and my startup company invented the paywall in the late ‘90s. I still feel bad about that. I’m trying to make up for it ever since.

But what I’ve been trying to think about with this is like, okay, right, first of all, we don’t necessarily know what the future is, and things are moving very quickly, and there’s every indication they’re going to keep moving quickly, even if LLMs start to even out, and there’s very divided opinions on when that will happen. But even if they do, there’s still so much low-hanging fruit of other stuff you could do with these systems that no one’s even bothered to attach them to yet, that we’ve got years of disruption ahead of us.

And so my advice has actually shifted a lot. Like, I mean, I’ve – Wharton runs a – I think we have the second highest number of startups of any school. Like, people raise lots of VC out of classes that I’ve taught and my colleagues have taught.

And I think one of the big shifts has been the idea that, like, there is an opportunity here that didn’t exist before. And part of my advice has shifted from, how do you help big companies succeed by providing software and services that they then buy you out for it, to like how do you smash large companies? Has become the new model for us.

I’m talking to venture capitalists. Increasingly, they’re telling me their startups are telling them they don’t want to grow past 20 people. Now, I don’t know if that’s going to be possible or not, right, but I think it’s an interesting change in ambition level from I want to grow as fast as I can to let’s see what we can do at scale, where scale is AI scale, not human scale.

And I think that that becomes a really interesting set of questions. And the question becomes like, what large companies can you solve better by having a thousand great servants? Because AI, you know, API calls are expensive at the level of like, if you’re replacing software calls. They’re very cheap if you’re using them to provide magic to people that otherwise humans would have had to provide. And so, part of that is that shift in that direction.

And then one other story just to tell you, like so I taught all my students how to build GPTs on OpenAI’s GPT platform. I know Microsoft has similar efforts that are coming out, too, that will make this even more broad, but they’re little agents, little software packages that you know, prompts basically.

And I had like 200 students in my class – in my classes. And the job was you had to come up with a way to destroy the job you were applying for, so that you could go for a job interview and hand someone your GPT and say, my job is done, give me a raise.

And I had like Navy pilot, I had hip-hop promoters, and of course, tons of like private equity folks at Wharton. And they did it, like they came up with ways of automating work that people have now adopted and used via the GPT store, like hundreds of different ways I never would have thought about before.

So I think there’s more low-hanging fruit than we’ve ever seen. I think the question is, how do you stay ahead of a technology that’s evolving? And I actually worry that a lot of startups are not being ambitious enough because they’re fixated on today. Like, how do I use a RAG-based system with, you know, using Llama 2 at the lowest possible cost? I’m like, I think you’re thinking the wrong way about this, right? You can’t be the person who’s conservative about where this technology is going. You have to have a viewpoint about what’s happening next.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, I – I think that’s super good advice. The thing I’ve been telling people for – like inside Microsoft for several years now and, you know, any of our partners that I’m chatting with right now is being incremental in this moment is like a horrible mistake. Like, the interesting things aren’t things that went from hard to easy, but the things that went from like, impossible to possible.

ETHAN MOLLICK: I love it.

KEVIN SCOTT: And like, you know, you sort of want to be right on the ragged frontier, you know, like, this thing’s barely possible, it’s too expensive, it’s like, super hard, it’s a little bit fragile, because all of that gets better. It gets cheaper. The fragile things become robust. It’s going to be extraordinary how all of this works.

ETHAN MOLLICK: And it would be better to bet on that happening than bet on it not happening, right? Like, we’re on a curve right now. And so, I’m – look, I’m paying attention, as is this the rest of the world, to how good the next version of OpenAI’s, you know, LLMs are going to be. Like, we’ll see, right? There’s certainly no expectation lowering happening from any of the major labs, you guys or anyone else. Like, so I’m going to keep betting that we’re going to see continued change, because I know that even if the technology stops developing, we’re still going to see continued change.

I’d like to see people do exactly that. Like, when I talk to companies, I always tell them two things. One thing is that something you used to do that was valuable is no longer valuable. And the sooner you realize that, the better off you are, right?

And the second thing is, there’s something impossible you always wanted to do that you can do. What is the impossible thing?

And I always tell people also, have something that is barely working. Like, I’d love something where the LLMs are just slightly incapable of doing it because you just wait for better brains, and then you’re the first people to take advantage of that. And I think that that’s really important also.

KEVIN SCOTT: Have you thought at all about, like just on a macro level, what markets get disrupted by this? Because it would be kind of unusual with a big technology transition like this for big things in the market not changing in substantial ways. Like, I know I’ve got my list of things that I’m predicting, but I’m always curious to hear what others are.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yeah. I mean, it is – you know, it’s hard, right? Because I think that a lot of the things – the shape of the disruption is going to be determined. We have agency over that, right? Like the technology is shifting, but we have a lot of agency over that.

And the model I always think about is the Industrial Revolution, and in a way people don’t usually think about, which is steam power came to a lot of factories in England at the same kind of time. The ones that won were not the ones that were like, hey, we could still make pots, but with less people. Those companies got destroyed, right? The ones that succeeded were the ones that said we can now use the same number of people and make 10,000 more pots and ship them all over the world, right?

KEVIN SCOTT: Yes.

ETHAN MOLLICK: And I think that that’s what we’re going to see happening is the disruption is going to be everywhere. Because look, I can make – using GPT-4, right, I can make a vending machine that is perfectly persuasive to you and solves your psychological problems, and, you know, it also sells Coke products, right? Like, what do we do with a world where that’s happening is a sort of weird question.

So I’m looking for imagination coming out of the large companies as an indicator that they’ll be safe, rather than looking at industries. I mean, look, I think we’re going to see disruption in customer service, but a really good version of this would say, how do we extend the customer service process to fit humans into it?

And I’m worried about companies not taking the visionary approach and instead just automation as cost cutting. That’s going to be dangerous. And those will be transformed in a negative way.

So I think we’re going to see this touch everywhere, but I think we have some agency over what happens. And obviously I think that the industries that matter the most are the most white collar kind of, you know, analytical jobs. Like, everyone could use a consultant to help them out, right? And so, the challenge facing BCG or Accenture is not how do I do, you know, serve our current clients with the same number of people. It’s what do I do now that I have increased capabilities, and one person can manage a thousand interns, and I want to see that happen.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah. I think that’s such a amazingly good point and a push. I think one of the things I wrote in my book is like, I don’t know whether there is the history of any company that somehow or another innovated its way into the future by focusing only on cutting costs. You know, and I do think that’s an important thing for enterprises. Like, you have to figure out how to innovate and serve your customers better, and to reinvent yourself over time and to respond to competition. And like all of that requires what you just said. Like, I’ve got tools and I’ve got human capital, and like, how can I put those two things together in like, really inventive ways to invent the future?

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yeah, and I – not to sound, and I want to make clear to listeners, I don’t take money from Microsoft or any other AI lab, but I think now is the time not to sound too Microsoft pro AI lab, but I think now’s the time not to worry so much about keeping your API costs as low as possible, and to view this stuff as R&D, right, for a future. I think we need to be thinking about, like, you know, you need to be doing R&D because there’s no instruction manual. And I think too many companies are kind of paralyzed and people are paralyzed waiting for like the secret recipe book, and there isn’t one, right?

Like I’ve talked to everyone building on. You do, too. It’s not like we know the full set of capabilities. If I told you how good is this for, you know, for helping people who are chemical engineers working on long distance pipe, you know, we don’t even know what their jobs are. But I guess, you know, the people who can figure that out are the people at chemical companies working on pipe engineering. They will know very quickly what it’s good or bad at.

And what I worry about is them pausing or waiting for a consultant to tell them the answer, because nobody has that answer. Your expertise is the key to multiplying your effect right now.

KEVIN SCOTT: I think the other, you know, interesting thing here is, you know, again, I do have a point of view about whether or not we’re reaching the point of diminishing marginal returns on model power. I don’t think we are. And you know, the structure of how these systems get built is you go build a gigantic supercomputer and then you go train a model on it, and then you release the model to the world, and then you start the whole cycle over again. And, like, they’re actually overlapped at this point, but it takes a while to, like, go build the supercomputers and then train the next model. And so, that doesn’t mean that you don’t have an exponentially improving process. It just means you’re sampling the exponential like once every, you know, couple of years.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yes, I love that.

KEVIN SCOTT: And you just can’t get confused that, you know, like you got the last sample and now everything is linear.

ETHAN MOLLICK: And I worry. I mean, I worry about that and I really do think, like, you know, I think that, you know, I get that people are worn out from tech cycles, right? Like, I think the NFT world did a lot of people in of like, you know, because it illustrated everything of a tech cycle. But like, the truth is we haven’t had a bust, right?

Like – and the companies I talk to are being transformed. Like, I haven’t spoken to anyone who’s done internal implementations of AI systems, who’s like, you know, this didn’t do anything for us. Like, I’m sure there’s plenty out there, but in terms of actually embracing it, building it, you know, into their systems and thinking about it, that they don’t find benefit, I’m just not hearing that very often. Most companies are just getting started and barely thinking about it.

And, you know, we’re not going to see evidence in productivity numbers for a while. Systems take much longer to change than technologies. But that doesn’t give you a license to ignore it.

And I think there’s no one who uses these systems for 10-plus hours. That’s why my borderline in my book is 10 hours of, you know, frontier, you know, copilot and, you know, in GPT-4 mode or GPT-4 quad three, you know, Gemini 1.5, whatever it is. You need to put 10 hours in with that. And if you are still unconvinced, I get it, right? But like, yeah, I don’t meet many people who aren’t.

KEVIN SCOTT: So how do you get people to be optimistic enough? And, like, the last thing that I would ever encourage anyone to do is to be blindly optimistic about anything, especially, you know, blindly optimistic about things where they’re investing a lot of their money or a lot of their precious time into. But this is one of those areas where you need just enough optimism and curiosity, where you are pushing. Otherwise, you know, I think you get yourself into a little bit of trouble, you know, just in the sense that, you know, people who lean all the way in and use the tool to its best effect are going to have big advantages over people who don’t.

And like, the only thing really, you know, given how cheap the tools are, that prevents you from leaning in is like your own psychology, you know, like your own, you know, willingness to go experiment and try.

I’ll tell you, like, just speaking about Microsoft, like it was an interesting thing when GPT-4 was first available, trying to get people to, like, go use it as ambitiously as they should, because, you know, they had all of the perfectly reasonable reasons for why they shouldn’t. And the longer everyone waited, like the – you know, the less well-off they were, like, relative to like, trying to accomplish the thing that they were trying to accomplish.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yeah. I mean, so, listen, I actually think you need to have a crisis in some ways. Like, I don’t mean to – like I am very optimistic, but I think you have to get through a crisis one way or another. Like I call it the three sleepless nights. Like, you have to have a moment where you’re like, oh my God, this feels like it’s not a person, it’s not alive, but it feels alive. It could do parts of my job I didn’t expect. Like, you have to kind of push past because there’s a lot of psychological resistance. There’s some weirded-out-ness. There is natural skepticism, overhype. There is anxiety. And I find a lot of really smart people that I talk to are like, I kind of walked away from it, right?

There’s also the fact that most people use one of the free models, and those aren’t as good, right? Again, if you’re going to use a free model, it has to be use copilot in creative GPT-4 mode. Like, you need to use a frontier model to figure out what’s going on.

And you need to do your 10 hours, and you’re going to have a crisis. Like, it is weird. It is something we’ve never kind of contacted another form of intelligence before. It’s a weird form of intelligence. It’s not human. It’s not sentient. It doesn’t have goals. But it will fake that all for you initially. And then once you get through it, you’ll be fine. But you have to get there. And I think it’s very uncomfortable and hard for people to push through.

And then afterwards you start to find liberating uses for this. Like, that’s what I tend to find is very few people seem to be continually upset afterwards because they start to find places where like, this sucks, I don’t want to do this part of my job, and the AI will handle it for me. And if you start with the high friction parts, you’re in good shape.

Now, there’s places where that’s bad. My students are like, too many people are cheating everywhere. They were already cheating, but now AI makes cheating even easier, right? And like, there are things we have to do that are hard things that are human things. But I think that pushing through and getting those 10 hours and getting to the crisis and just realizing it’s something you have to go through, like this is a – there’s a change happening, and it’s exciting, but it’s also unnerving in a deep way.

And, you know, one of the things I’ve talked to people at Microsoft is like, how do we help people both go through the crisis but then feel positive afterwards. And I don’t think we have an easy answer to that set of questions. But I do think that the discomfort is part of the process.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, I totally agree. I mean, almost everyone that I know, like both optimists and pessimists, have had the crisis that you are talking about. And like, funny enough, the optimist crisis is really that the fundamental mechanism of these transformer-based models isn’t that complicated? I mean, there’s a huge amount of complicated stuff underlying it. And like, I’ve seen people have the flavor of crisis, which is like, all right, like I actually understand the mechanism of this thing. How on God’s earth is it getting in the ballpark of things that I thought I was good at? Like, does that mean that I’m a stochastic parrot? Does it, you know, it’s really interesting like watching that.

ETHAN MOLLICK: That is 100% one of the most common forms of this is, oh, my God, are we just this, right? And I think you come to the conclusion that we’re not just this –

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, correct.

ETHAN MOLLICK: But something like this is part of what we do. But that’s not that – that, you know, it’s sort of like saying being a biochemist, you know everything about how a person works because, you know biochemistry.

Like, but there is a crisis there, right? There is a – nothing was close to us this way. It’s like finding out that, you know, that dolphins really can talk and they’ve been talking about you behind your back the whole time. Like, there is something startling about this.

But I think that, you know, it is reconstructable afterwards. I think there’s lots of open questions about why is it so good, how does it compare to the human brain? But I think the kind of naive view is probably wrong, right? It’s not – we’ve found something that works kind of like – I’ve always liked Stephen Wolfram’s kind of argument that there is a statistical pattern to language, which means statistical patterns of thought, and you can reproduce that without necessarily thinking. But it’s tough. We don’t have the answers to these questions really yet.

KEVIN SCOTT: No, we don’t. Yeah. These systems are ill understood. Our own cognition is ill understood. Like, it’s, you know, it’s an interesting, interesting set of vagaries here. But like if you can sort of look at the thing as like it’s just a tool, like I’m going to try to figure out what the tool is good for, then you can do interesting things with it.

ETHAN MOLLICK: And I think that that tool analogy is important, right? It’s many things. Like, it’s weird because you have to both keep thinking about it as a tool, but you also have to treat it like a person to get the best results from it. So it’s hard for people to keep both those things in their head at the same time.

But the problem is that both analogies are kind of bad, right? Like, I hear privacy concerns from companies all the time that don’t make a lot of sense, because they’re privacy concerns you wouldn’t have about any other tool in the cloud that everyone’s using all the time, right? So it’s very weird to see people being panicked, like, but it knows everything. I’m like, well, I mean, are you worried that Dropbox is stealing all of your data? You’re not, right?

So part of this is this kind of conversation about like, oh, they’re thinking like an entity that’s learning stuff. So if you show it something, everybody’s learning the thing you’re showing it, and it’s not working that way either.

So it’s a little tough to keep both things in your mind at one time, but you kind of have to. And I think once you use it, a little bit of the magic wears off at the same time, but the usefulness increases. And that’s a good place to be.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah. So what are some of your concerns about the technology?

ETHAN MOLLICK: So I think I’ve kind of – I mean, there is a bunch of baked-in bad stuff that’s going to happen one way or another, because I don’t think it’s stoppable at this point. Like, we have a perfect phishing machine. The world is going to get filled up with AI slop, right? Or anything since 2022 is going to be contaminated with AI information. You know, and I use contaminated in both good and bad ways, right? Like, we have a different information ecosystem than we had before. No matter what we do to do watermarking or other approaches, open source solutions that are out there that are powerful enough that they’re going to get around whatever barriers are put in place and that die is cast, right?

So the nature of IT security is going to have to change fairly dramatically. The nature of how we treat online information is going to have to change fairly dramatically. There is going to be some escalation of scams. And I mean, there’s bad stuff that’s going to happen that’s almost 100% certain going to happen.

You know, and there’s social changes that are going to be, you know, the job disruptions that I think we will see one way or another. The fact that people are going to make AI friends, right, like this is going to be something we’re going to have to deal with. And we don’t know whether or not it becomes one of these things that at some point people are like, okay, I’ve had enough, I want to have like real friends too, or whether there’s – this is going to be too compelling. AI is very persuasive.

Like, these are already baked-in changes, and I think we need to start mitigating negative effects. What I’m worried about is sometimes we focus too much on AI will wake up and murder us all, and not so much on what do we do to regulate a machine and a thing we’ve seen before, which is a machine that can do a lot of things that are good and bad. How do we mitigate the bad effects, while allowing the good effects to happen?

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah.

ETHAN MOLLICK: So I think that’s my real concern.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, and you know, I think the encouraging thing from the historical record is like, we’ve got plenty of expertise at figuring out how to regulate technologies that are ubiquitous.

Like one of my favorite examples is electricity. So electricity in many different ways is a potentially lethal technology. And, you know, we regulate how it’s generated. We regulate how it’s transmitted. We regulate how it is terminated and connected to your house. We regulate the appliances that consume it. Like, we regulate the people who work on all of the different systems. And, you know, and still even then, like a small number of tens of thousands of people in the United States die from accidental electrocution every year. And we have all of that, and like it is completely unimaginable to everyone how you would live your life without electricity. And there are many other technologies like that.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Yeah. I mean, I think we’ll figure it out, right? I mean, part of this is we have to figure it out. And one of the things that I like the sort of policy implications that Josh Gans, who’s a professor at UC Toronto, has been saying, which is, look, when you don’t know benefits or risks, what you need is fast, smart, responsive regulation where you look for emerging harms and then you regulate, right?

And so, we know there’s emerging harms around identity and there’s emerging harms around fakes. You know, like we should be thinking about how we’re solving those policy concerns.

I think that it’s worthwhile to think about the sort of danger scenario of what happens if we do have a sentient machine that’s smarter than all humans. But I feel like compared to the other kinds of risks that we know are happening and the other benefits, like micro regulation is actually really needed.

When I talk to industries, right, one of the major problems like financial services has is they’re not clear what the regulation is. So they can’t act as much as they want to, because the regulation was written for algorithmic forms of AI, where the decision was about AI decision-making, you know, in FICO scores and things like that. And that’s not really relevant to LLMs.

So we need positive regulation too that carves out room to experiment. How does this work in medicine? What are HIPAA requirements? Like, the people who are sort of anti-regulation, also we live in a system that’s already regulated. Like, there’s already choices that have been made.

KEVIN SCOTT: And it’s a thing worth reminding folks, like there’s plenty of regulation already that applies to AI. Like, you know, AI can’t write prescriptions; only doctors can. And, you know, even there, like, someone will have to figure out, like whether or not and to what extent a doctor can take a recommendation from an AI and use that to write a prescription for a medicine that goes to a patient. Yeah, AI models are not FDA 510(k) compliant. So, like, you can’t use them as a medical device. So like there’s just a bunch of regulation right now, and some of it actually you may need to relax because the benefits are going to be so blaringly really obvious for the public, it will help us solve so many problems, you’re probably going to want to change the regulation even in the other direction to, like, make things more permissive than they are right now.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Fast experimentation is going to be key. So we need to figure out ways to allow fast experimentation to happen. But it’s also fair that like there’s going to be downside risks everywhere. It’s a general purpose technology, right? Those come along once every generation or so. And you know, computers plus internet was the last one. And turns out we needed to put a lot of regulation in place about how that operates. But we also had to allow experimentation.

And I think it’s important to view this as a technology with you know, there are enough smart people who think there’s substantial world-ending risk that I want to take that seriously, but I’m much more worried about, you know, in the short term, I don’t think there’s enough discussion about how we allow the good stuff to thrive and the bad stuff to stop and what that looks like. And, you know, how do we let experiments happen the right way but regulate quickly? That feels, to me, like a much more important question to have answers to than sort of, you know, than we’re giving attention to.

KEVIN SCOTT: Cool. So, looking forward to the next handful of years, like what are you most excited about?

ETHAN MOLLICK: I have been trying to teach education at scale for a long time. I started working at the Media Lab on this back in the mid-2000s. I worked on DARPA projects on using games for training at scale. I’ve been building tools to teach business skills.

It turns out something we don’t teach enough is like you teach someone how to network or how to pitch or any of this stuff, and like their life changes actually in controlled studies. Like, it’s amazing. Small amounts of skills because we don’t actually teach those to most people, and they’re very useful, right?

And so, I’ve been building games and simulations to teach at scale for a really long time and watching like what happens. Like, I’ve got these massive online video courses that a couple hundred thousand people have taken. And, you know, as much as I like, the MOOCs are a terrible way to teach. You’re watching a bunch of passive videos.

So to me, the education stuff is such a low-hanging piece of, you know, fruit here, which is, you told me with your daughter already moving ahead of class, we’re going to have to start relaxing some of our ideas of teaching. But it’s going to be great also for teachers because, I mean, it’s very funny when I hear teachers feel threatened in some way because, you know, I’m a teacher, too.

By the way, you know, business school professor is going to be one of the most disruptive jobs on all of the O*NET lists, right? It’s number 22 out of 1,016 jobs of most disrupted jobs.

But disruption doesn’t mean destruction. I have to teach hundreds of kids in my classes. I don’t get to know all of them because I can’t, right? I can’t reach all of them. When I teach a classroom, I’m a pretty good teacher, but I kind of have to teach to the middle or the high end of the class. What happens if you’re stuck? Suddenly we have systems that can help bring everyone up to speed, that can help us dynamically develop learning environments that work, that can be individual tutors. Like, to me, this is undeniably exciting, and it’s ready. Like, we can get there with today’s technology. And to me, like that’s a burning need.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, I totally agree. And like I think it’s up and down the stack. Like, you look at the number of kids who don’t have access to the full set of educational resources that could help them flourish, it’s in the developing world, it’s in, you know, school systems in part of the country that are just sort of underinvested in for a whole variety of reasons. And like, honestly, it’s even the elite institutions.

So, you know, I look at some of these elite schools where they’re like, oh, we only admit 5% of our applicants or, you know, 4% of our applicants. And I’m like, that’s a just appalling thing because, you know, everybody who applies to MIT, I mean, like, not everybody, but like some crazy proportion of them are like, outstandingly talented, and like the fact that you got to draw the line, you know, like it’s kind of nutty.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Talent is much more evenly distributed than opportunity. And our biggest cost is that, right? There are talented people all over the world who don’t have access to opportunity. And like, the fact that we can close any of that gap will be a net benefit to everybody.

And like listen, I’ve been working in education for a long time, and there was a shift from sort of utopian technological solutions, right, like one laptop per child and all of these, like we just give people technology and everyone thrives, which kind of comes from our ‘80s nerd that we mentioned earlier, which was like, oh, we learned how to code by just being given a laptop; everybody should.

It turns out that’s not true. There’s social systems in place. There’s problems at all levels of society. There’s been a big shift to thinking about those things, which I think are really important.

But I actually think we’ve kind of lost a little bit of the technological optimism of, like, hey, maybe there are tools that can help solve these problems, and it doesn’t have to all be like the most grinding social change.

And that’s what excites me the most is like restoring a bit of that optimistic piece, while still worrying about downside risks. For example, the default, all the models right now, if you ask them to teach, they all want to use learning styles, which is actually 95% of teachers believe they’re real, but it’s debunked as a learning method. Like you don’t actually learn better when you’re taught – if you’re a visual learner, you should not be just taught visually actually. It makes you a worse student because you should be learning in multi-modes, and learning styles don’t really work. But the AI has absorbed this because that’s what most people think. So we have to tell the AI, don’t use learning styles when we build a tutor.

So it’s not like you can use this stuff completely unsupervised, but at the same time, it’s pretty good out of the box and best available human. Is it better than what you have access to right now?

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah,  100%.

Cool. Well, maybe a couple more questions before we go. So maybe this isn’t even an AI question. I’m sort of interested in what’s on the mind of your students. So, like they’re entering a really interesting world right now. And like, you know, AI notwithstanding, like what’s on their mind as they’re approaching the future? Like, what are they anxious about and what are they, you know, optimistic for?

ETHAN MOLLICK: I mean, I’m seeing entrepreneurship continue to be really exciting for a lot of them. I mean, I think that the question everyone asks me all the time is like, well, how will jobs change, and, you know, what should I do?

And I say, you know, I think we’ve all learned don’t try and predict any individual job. I mean, there are some fields that I think are riskier than others, but, you know, they’re already having issues, right? Journalism is in decline, sadly, for – you know, for a variety of reasons that have nothing to do with AI.

But, you know, I don’t tell people to switch jobs because of this, right, and I think the question is figure out what you’re good at more than anything else and double down on that.

So I think part of this is the uncertainty. I mean, but it’s been uncertain times for very long time, right? Like I started teaching right after the Great Recession started, right?, and the collapse of the housing market. There’s not really that many times where everyone’s like, yes, everything is super stable right now.

So my students have panicked from time immemorial and they seem to do really well. The thing I’m also trying to tell them is like, the one thing that’s definitely happened is careers are now long. You’ll do many things over your career. You shouldn’t over-index on your first job, your first thing you’re doing. Maybe you stick with it right? Maybe you have a long career there. But like, people move, jobs change, the world changes, and you’re going to be okay. Stay flexible. You know, make luck work on your side as best you can. And I think most people work out, you know, everyone has setbacks, but there’s ways forward in most cases.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah. All right, last question. So you’re just super busy right now. You know, I think before we hit record, you know, both of us were talking about all of the many things that we’re off, you know, doing that are supplements to, I guess, our day job. But like, given how busy you are and how interesting the work is that you’re doing right now on AI, what do you do in your free time? Like, what’s your passion?

ETHAN MOLLICK: So, you know, family and I are really close. We do all kinds of fun trips and doing stuff together. My solo activity is I’m a gamer. And that’s my sort of stress relief and kind of fun. I do a lot of, like these very hard kinds of games called roguelike games that I happen to really enjoy, that – where you lose most of the time. And I find that a really interesting randomness plus trial, plus figuring out how you solve things. So I do a lot of games, a lot of gaming.

And it sort of runs right into AI, because what I end up doing is kind of combining the two a lot. I like to figure out how the AI could do something. Thinking about how AI as a game designer is a really interesting approach to working with it.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, that’s super, super cool.

Well thank you so much for taking time out of your schedule to chat with us and thank you for being a optimistic voice. Like, I think the thing that you are doing is genuinely helpful right now. I think getting people excited enough to, like, give this stuff a try is going to help them do super interesting things in the future. So like that encouragement is really essential, I think.

ETHAN MOLLICK: That’s great. I mean, and the funny thing is, I feel like I’m a pragmatist, right? I just think that the voices of non-optimism kind of get drowned out. And so like, I’m never saying like, oh, do this at all costs. We’ve just talked about regulation. Like, I don’t think this is like we need to push every lever forward. But like, how can we not look at some of these changes and not be excited for what they can bring? And how – especially because we have agency now. Like, this is the time to shape what these things look like.

And that’s an exciting time to be, right? Like, we don’t get to choose the moment we’re born, right? This is an interesting moment, and I hope people see that and see that they can shape their world a little bit in their own image.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, well, that is a beautiful note to end this on. Thank you very much for being with us today.

ETHAN MOLLICK: Thank you for having me.

CHRISTINA WARREN: Yes. Yeah, no, same. And I – as somebody who’s always loved automation and things like that. That’s why, you know, this wave has been so exciting.

But no, your point, yeah, it’s usually not the exciting stuff. But yet I think, almost paradoxically, and you see this with Ethan’s work and the things that he shares, it’s like that’s what unlocks the potential because it’s like, okay, if we can do this, then we can start imagining other things that this can help enhance, which is really powerful.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah, for sure. And like just in general, I think the energy that he’s radiating right now feels to me a lot like the energy that lots and lots of people were radiating during the PC revolution and the internet wave. I mean, it was such an exciting time, and I feel like we’re back there again, that this is yet another one of those uniquely exciting times where a new thing has come on the scene, it has so much possibility and potential in it. And like, if you are curious and ambitious and, you know, like are willing to go build on top of the thing, like you’re going to be able to do some interesting stuff. And some of it will fail and like, we’ll learn in failure, and like some of it will, like, really make a difference, I think.

CHRISTINA WARREN: No, I completely agree. And one of the things that I appreciate very much about Ethan is that, as you mentioned before, like he’s trying these things out. He’s encouraging other people to try these things out. So that, I think, also helps, you know, make people more comfortable. Okay, well, let me play this, let me try this out, which is, I think, you can correct me if I’m wrong on this, but that’s a little bit different than what we’ve had in, in previous revolutions, because the barrier to entry now is more accessible than it’s been before.

KEVIN SCOTT: Oh, 100 percent.

CHRISTINA WARREN: And – which, you know, you talked about your daughter and how she’s using things, right? Like, this is everyone has access to – well, not everyone, but we’re working on making these tools accessible to as many people as possible. And that, I think, you know, means that the excitement that can come around this and the education, as Ethan was talking about, that can come around this has so much potential, separate from – from the technological advancements.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yeah. I mean, I do think the power of the things that people have access to that are literally, freely available, as long as you’ve got an internet connection and access to a computer, and like, those are like two big things that we shouldn’t take as given. Like, we still live in a world where, like a lot of us probably do take it for a given, like internet access and having a computer, like whether it’s a smartphone or a PC, like not everyone has, by a long shot.

CHRISTINA WARREN: For sure.

KEVIN SCOTT: But if you do have those things, like if you’re connected, like the power of the tools that you have free access to is just extraordinary. Like, they have never been more powerful or giving you more capability to do more things. And like that to me is exciting. So, you know, more people have these superpowers now than have ever had them before.

CHRISTINA WARREN: Absolutely. And I’m glad that we have leaders and educators like Ethan who are out there helping encourage and unpack what these things mean, and, and you know, help propel things forward.

KEVIN SCOTT: Yep, agree.

CHRISTINA WARREN: All right, well, that is all the time that we have for today. Huge thanks to Ethan Mollick for joining us.

If there’s anything that you’d like to share with us, you can email us at behindthetech@microsoft.com. And you can follow Behind the Tech on your favorite podcast platform. Or you can check out our full video experience on YouTube. Thanks for listening.

KEVIN SCOTT: See you next time.
